And he shall rest between his shoulders. Burdens are carried upon the shoulders, and therefore in them sturdy patience is designated. And the more gladly did Christ rest in him, the harder were the labours he bore for his sake: the more closely was he embraced, the less was he torn from that embrace by any terror. Whence he says: Who shall separate us from the charity of Christ? (Rom. VIII.)

VERS. 13.-- To Joseph also he said. Joseph is interpreted increased. This is Christ, who, cast off by his brethren the Jews, was made prince in the Egypt of this world, and freed the human race from the want of famine by the dispensation of the evangelical 497Dwheat: who is truly the Saviour of the world.

Of the blessing of the Lord be his land. To the letter, it signifies the fertility which the two tribes out of Joseph, that is, Ephraim and Manasses, had in abundance of crops, of fruits, and of pasture for their flocks. This is Basan, a most fertile region, whence it is interpreted fatness.

By the mildness of the sky, and by dew, the fruits, and the abundance of springs and of rivers, are delighted with man, which things are signified in the deep.

Thou hast blessed, O Lord, thy land, namely the Church, which is filled divinely, not by its own power, but by the blessing of the virtues. Whence: Blessed be God, 498Awho hath blessed us with every spiritual blessing in heavenly things in Christ (Ephes. I).

Of the heavens. The apostles and evangelists are called heaven because of the unity of faith and of doctrine, of whom it is said elsewhere in the plural: The heavens declare the glory, etc. (Psal. XVIII.) For in them, shining with life and contemplation, God dwells as in heaven, thundering terror, raining down consolation, and flashing with miracles.

Of the dew. Of heavenly preaching, which is of wondrous subtlety and grace, by which hearts are watered against the heat of temptation, and that they may always be green with virtues; whence: God give thee of the dew of heaven, etc. (Gen. XXVII.)

Of the deep, namely that it may become the fatter, bedewed from the spring that rises up, like the paradise of God. Whence: A spring rose 498Bout of the earth, watering the whole surface of the earth (Gen. II). From the sea his understanding abounded, and his thought from the great deep. For in vain does the word of the teacher water from without, unless the grace of the Creator waters from within. Whence he subjoins: Of the fruits of the hills, etc.

VERS. 14.-- Of the sun. Of Christ, who is the sun of justice, who says: The just shall shine as the sun in the kingdom of their Father (Matth. XIII), because we shall be like to him.

And of the moon. Of the Church, which receives these fruits from the sun, that is, from Christ: let her therefore always look upon the sun with full devotion, lest at any time, turned away, she lose the light.

VERS. 15.-- Of the fruits of the everlasting hills. The everlasting hills are the faithful of the Old Testament, humble sons founded with unmoved firmness upon the faith of the prophets and patriarchs (unde: Mons domus Domini elevabitur super colles (Isa. II) ), with whose fruits the land of Joseph 498Cis blessed, when the Church bears fruit in faith by the doctrine of Christ's prophecy and by the example of virtue. And it is to be noted that above, in the fruits and the dew of heaven, evangelical doctrine is signified, here, in the top of the ancient mountains and the fruits of the everlasting hills, the legal and prophetic; and by both the land of Joseph is enriched, because holy Church is formed by the pages of both Testaments and by the examples of the new Fathers and the old; whence: All fruits, my beloved, that is, the new and the old, I have kept for thee (Cant. VII), and: Every scribe instructed bringeth forth out of his treasure new things and old (Matth. XIII). The Manichaeans therefore and the Jews (because the former do not receive the Old, the latter the New Testament) cannot inherit in this rich land.

498DVERS. 16.-- And of the fruits. The Church, fruitful in the whole world with the fruit of justice and with the largess of the charisms, has received of the fulness of her Joseph; whence there follows:

The blessing of him who appeared. The whole Joseph, that is, head and body, is full of blessing, but the fulness of the blessing is chiefly in the head; whence: Like the ointment on the head, which ran down upon the beard of Aaron, etc. (Psal. XXXII.) He, pre-eminent over his brethren, that is, over the faithful, by the privilege of grace, shone forth with the sevenfold Spirit as a true Samson, a Nazarite from the womb of the Virgin, that is, consecrated to God, as it were with his seven locks untouched.

Upon the head of Joseph, of Christ, who is the head of the Church, in whom all nations are blessed, of whose 499Apassion and resurrection it is subjoined: As of the firstborn of a bull, etc.

As. (AUG., quaest. 56.) The firstborn of a bull is his beauty. It is not to be read in such a way that he is called the firstborn of a bull: but, since he is the firstborn, his beauty is the beauty of a bull, because of the horns of the cross.

Christ is the fatted calf, whom the Father sacrificed for the returning Son as the firstborn out of the herd of the fathers, who on the third day was raised up and appeared in glory, who is the firstborn of the dead and the prince of the kings of the earth, and who after the glory of the resurrection adds horns out of the power of the passion.

VERS. 17.-- Horns. He compares Joseph to the horns of a rhinoceros, signifying that Ephraim would have the chief place among the ten tribes, over which Jeroboam 499Bof the tribe of Ephraim reigned in the time of Roboam the son of Solomon.

The horns of a rhinoceros. It signifies the horns of the cross, of which Habacuc says: There is his strength hidden (Habac. III). For through the weakness of the flesh he is crowned, like a ram, with the thorns of the mockers, and by these horns he stuck fast; but by the incomparable strength of the passion he stood forth a unicorn, the conqueror of death, and of him who had the empire of death, whence: That which is the weak thing of God is stronger than men (I Cor. I)

He shall winnow. By the preaching of the cross, separating the chaff from the wheat: for the word of the cross is foolishness to them that perish.

The nations. Ask of me, and I will give thee the nations, etc. (Psal. II.) For of the inheritance of the true Joseph (which is 499Cthe glorious land) all these things are said; and of its tillers, gathered out of both peoples, there follows:

These are the multitudes of Ephraim. These two tribes out of Joseph are blessed not in themselves, but in their parent. They signify the sons of both peoples, who are blessed in Christ, according to that word: In thy seed shall all nations be blessed (Gen. XXII). These the patriarch Jacob, blessing Ephraim and Manasses with his hands crossed in the manner of a cross, and preferring the younger to the firstborn, signified were to be blessed in the faith of Christ's cross, marking in the younger the people faithful from among the gentiles, lesser in time within the grace of Christ, but greater in faith: to whom the grace of the people of Israel passed over, of whose inestimable multitude it is said: These are the multitudes, etc., whence: Many are the children of the desolate, more than of her 499Dthat hath a husband (Isa. LIV).

Manasses, deprived of the honour of the firstborn and yet blessed, is the Jewish people, which for the greater part of itself was reprobated for unbelief, yet in the remainder obtained the blessing, whence: Blindness in part happeneth in Israel, etc. (Rom. II.)

VERS. 18.-- And to Zabulon. Zabulon and Issachar received their passion in Galilee, where Christ chiefly taught, whence he chose the apostles. Through them therefore the apostles are designated, and to them gladness is promised in their going out, because going out from their own borders they subjected the people of the gentiles to Christ, and in their tabernacles for the conversion of the Jews, from whom they themselves also were chosen. For both peoples therefore gladness is promised them, present and to come: 500Abecause in the present they rejoiced over their conversion, and in the heavenly places they were rewarded with eternal gladness.

In the tabernacles, of the Jews, whence: May God enlarge Japheth, and may he dwell in the tents of Sem (Gen. IX): because in the churches of the Jews there dwells the breadth of the lesser people through the unity of faith.

VERS. 19.-- To the mountain. Sion, namely, or Jerusalem, or contemplation and the height of the virtues.

They shall call. Teaching that justice is only in the faith of Christ. For the end of the law is Christ, unto justice to everyone that believeth (Rom. X). On the mountain they sacrifice the victims of justice, while they teach that all who come to Christ (in whom the Church is built) are justified not by the works of the law but by the faith of Christ, according to that word: But the just 500Bman liveth by faith (Habac. I). True Christians therefore are the victims of justice, which are sacrificed by the apostles, while they are taught to mortify earthly desires, that spiritual things may be made alive; whence: If by the Spirit you mortify the deeds of the flesh, you shall live (Rom. VIII).

There they shall sacrifice. There is no place of true sacrifice outside the catholic Church, which is set upon this mountain.

Who suck the flood. These tribes gave themselves to trafficking, bringing back merchandise from regions across the sea, and hidden treasures, silver, namely, gold or gems, they shall suck as milk, that is, they shall receive them easily.

Who suck the flood of the sea. It signifies the calling of the gentiles. For the sea signifies the whole race of men; 500Cwhence: The kingdom of heaven is like to a net cast into the sea, and gathering together of all kinds of fishes (Matth. XIII). By the sea, the world; by the net, doctrine; by every kind of fishes, every sex, age and condition is understood. The apostles therefore sucked the flood of the sea as milk, because they drew the throngs of the peoples flowing together to the doctrine of the Gospel into the increase of Christ's body.

Hidden. The Lord knoweth who are his (I Tim. II). For many are called, few, etc. (Matth. XX.)

The sands are the multitude of the unbelieving; the treasures, the precious souls of the elect. So therefore did they suck, that of the hearers of the Gospel some were carried over as vessels of honour into the treasures of the king, while others remained empty, like barren and 500Duseless sands.

VERS. 20.-- And to Gad he said. In the blessing of Gad he seems to foretell the might of the strong men, or the judgeship, who were of that tribe, such as Jephte, and many others.

In breadth. Because all the fulness of blessing rested upon Christ, whence he blessed his own.

Gad. That is, girded, signifies Christ, who, being about to fight against spiritual wickednesses, appeared in the weakness of the flesh girded with the power of the Godhead, according to that word: The Lord is clothed with strength and hath girded himself (Psal. CXII). And elsewhere: The Lord strong and mighty in battle, etc. He is blessed in breadth, because not among the Jews alone, but among all 501Anations he received a most ample kingdom. Whence the Prophecy says:

From the rising of the sun unto the going down thereof, the name of the Lord is worthy of praise. But because he came to this glory through the humility of the passion, he subjoins concerning his death:

He rested as a lion. I have power to lay down my life (Joan. X). The power of death, not the necessity, is expressed; whence: No man taketh it away from me, but I lay it down. (Ibid.)

He roared as a lion. For whatsoever he said, he powerfully fulfilled.

And he took the arm and the head. When, rising from the dead, he subjected to himself the strength of his enemies. He took also the head, that is, he bent the loftiness of worldly empire to the receiving of the faith; whence: 501BAnd all kings shall adore him; all nations shall serve him (Psal. LXXI). There follows concerning the new state of his kingdom.

VERS. 21.-- And he saw his principality, that in his own portion. When kingdoms and nations had been subjected to him, his power appeared, not by the terror of arms, but by the strength of doctrine.

Laid up. Hidden, while he writes his laws in the hearts of the faithful; whence he says: You have the unction from the Holy One, and you have no need that any man teach you; his unction teacheth you of all things (Joan. II). Or, Laid up, because in the law and the prophets he lay hidden in figures, and taught while concealed the things which he declared openly in the Gospel; whence: Beginning from Moses and all the prophets, he expounded to them (Luc. XXIV).

501CWho was with the princes of the people. A helper, whence: The Lord working withal and confirming the word with signs that followed (Marc. XVI), because he says: Without me you can do nothing (Joan. XV).

He did justices. That is, justifying those who believe in him, and at length he will judge according to their merits with Israel, that is, with his people chosen for the vision of God.

Justices. The justifications of believers. For he himself justifies the ungodly out of faith. For neither shall any flesh be justified by the works of the law.

VERS. 22.-- Dan also. Others think that by Dan is signified Antichrist, whom they reckon will be born of this tribe: he is the whelp of the lion, that is, of the devil, who shall flow plentifully from Basan, because he is full of confusion; 501Dfor Basan is interpreted confusion, or fatness, or the locust.

Dan also means judgment, Basan ignominy. But Dan signifies Christ, who says: For judgment I am come into this world (Joan. IX). The whelp of a lion, because of his strength, and because he deigned to become a little one; but taking upon himself our weakness, he did not lose his own power, for that which is the weak thing of God is stronger than men (I Cor. I). Of him it is said: The lion of the tribe of Juda hath conquered, etc. (Apoc. V.) The ignominy of the cross indeed he bore from the Jews, who thought that his name would be blotted out by death: but he who through the weakness of that flesh was a whelp in the passion, appeared through the power of the Godhead an unconquered lion in the resurrection; whence the Apostle: And although he was crucified through weakness, 502Ayet he liveth by the power of God (II Cor. XIII). He flowed plentifully, therefore, as it were out of Basan, when, coming through the ignominy of death to the glory of immortality, he watered the whole world with the streams of the Gospel; whence Isaias: The earth is filled with the knowledge of the Lord, as the covering waters of the sea (Isa. II).

Dan is a lion's whelp. This he says because of Samson, who was of the tribe of Dan, and strong as a lion, whose possession reached as far as Basan. But Antichrist shall flow plentifully from Basan, because he shall be sated with the fatness of luxury and of all the vices.

He shall possess the sea and the south. Because his possession reaches from the southern quarter westward as far as the Tyrrhenian sea.

He shall possess the sea and the south. For through the apostles 502Bthe Church is won for Christ out of Jews and gentiles. For by the western sea (which is here understood) the Jews are figured, who serve the shadow and the copy, and have not the light of knowledge and of truth; and because for them the sun of justice has set, they have remained in perpetual darkness. This west the apostles possessed, when out of them they subjected to Christ very many enlightened by the grace of faith, that they who once were darkness might be light in the Lord. By the south is understood the Church gathered out of the gentiles, which, the veil of the law being taken away, enjoys the light of the Gospel, and lying down with the bridegroom at noon, contemplates with unveiled face the glory of the Lord.

VERS. 24.-- To Aser also he said: Blessed be Aser among his sons, let him be pleasing to his brethren. Aser, blessed, that is, Christ, the hope of our beatitude, whose firstfruits he showed 502Cto us in rising from the dead. He is blessed among his sons, because believers, born again through his grace, have filled the whole world with his likeness; to whom it is said: Believe in the light, sons, that you may be of the light (Joan. XII). They bring forth the fruit of light in all goodness and justice and truth. And this was done because he was pleasing with true charity to his brethren, that is, to the apostles, whom he chose out of Judea, so that, the figures being despised, they might rejoice in the truth of the Gospel, and because they were accounted worthy to suffer reproach for the name of Jesus. And whence they had so great an abundance of charity, he adds, let him dip his foot in oil, etc.

And let him dip his foot in oil. Christ our head, ascending into heaven, anointed most abundantly with the oil of the Holy Spirit the apostles — his feet, who were to go about the world preaching, while still standing on the earth 502D— that they might fail through no labour, but be strong with the brightness of unfading joy. He furnished also the incorruptible defence of a shoe, of which it is subjoined:

Iron and brass cal. (GREG., lib. XXXIV Moral., cap. 5.) This concerning the Church, etc., as far as with these shoes the true pasch is to be celebrated.

VERS. 25.-- As the days of thy youth. It signifies that this tribe abounded in prosperity and delights until the old age of the captivity.

As. To the Church is promised a raw and green old age: that as in the beginning she strongly overcame all assaults, so at the end that is at hand she may overcome the fury of Antichrist, whence: Behold I am with you all days 503Aeven to the consummation of the world (Matth. XXVIII). And elsewhere: My old age in plentiful mercy (Psalmus XCI).

VERS. 26.-- There is no God. Marvelling at the things said above, in praise of Christ, who did all these things, he breaks out full of gladness.

Who rideth upon the heaven. By his own power he ascended, and daily draws the Church to himself. Draw me after thee, run in the odour of thy ointments (Cant. I).

The clouds. The apostles, who rain with words, flash with miracles: whom, ascending into heaven, he sent to preach; whence: The clouds gave forth a voice (Psal. LXXVI), which run to and fro with none to delay them, working signs and wonders not by their own power but by Christ's, and terrifying the hearts of mortals as with lightnings, whence: 503BI have fought a good fight, I have finished my course (II Tim. IV).

VERS. 27.-- His dwelling place is above, etc. The body raised from the dead and placed at the right hand of the Father, of which it is said: Destroy this temple (Joan. II).

The arms. The power, namely, of the passion, which defends all, and which he received on the cross with arms outstretched.

Underneath the arms. These arms, namely, defend those who fight against the true Amalec: nor are they weighed down with weariness, as Moses's were, but lift up with unwearied strength, until they obtain the true victory. Let us venerate therefore in the highest the firstfruits of our resurrection, in the lowest the sacrament of our redemption, that we ourselves also, the adversary being overthrown, may obtain the glory of immortality; concerning which it is promised to the true Israel: He shall cast out 503Cthe enemy from before thy face.

And alone. Living, namely, after his own manner and by his own laws, whence Balaam: The people shall dwell alone, and shall not be reckoned among the nations (Num. XXIII).

VERS. 28.-- The eye of Jacob. He who here sweated to supplant the vices, there shall unceasingly contemplate, with the lightest glance of the mind, the glory of Christ, who for our sake took the earth of true flesh: so that he by whose body and blood he was here fed through the sacrament of wheat and wine, may there be filled full with the perpetual vision of his divinity, whence: I shall be satisfied when thy glory shall appear (Psal. XVI). And elsewhere: I will manifest myself to him (Joan. XIV). Then Jacob, turned into perfect Israel, shall see God face to face, with a knowledge of God so ample, so subtle, so delightful, filled as it were 503Dwith the lightest dew, that the knowledge of the preachers by which we are instructed in the present may seem to grow dark; whence: Or knowledge shall be destroyed (I Cor. XIII). And elsewhere: Dark water in the clouds of the air, before the brightness in his sight (Psal. XVII); and when it shall have been come to, all this doctrine shall cease, and they shall all be taught of God (Joan. VI); whence he rightly adds: Blessed art thou, Israel, etc

And the heavens shall be darkened, as though the dew were poured out so abundantly for the making fruitful of the earth, that the sight of men should grow dark. In which it is signified that the Jews, since they loved earthly wealth beyond measure, were darkened, and could not know Christ; whence: If we let him alone so, all will believe in him, etc.

504AVERS. 29.-- Who is like to thee, O people? As if to say: therefore blessed, therefore none is like to thee, because thou hopest for salvation not from thy own strength, but from the grace of Christ, who saveth his people from their sins.

And the sword of glory. In the world to come striking the adversaries with irrevocable severity, when he shall avenge the blood of his servants. The sword is now hidden here in the lowliness of those who suffer; it shall be revealed in the glory of those who rise again, and therefore it is called the sword of glory of Israel. Christ therefore as a shield protects the one who fights, and as a sword of glory shall avenge the one who triumphs. To him, as to the head, the glory of the body is referred at the end of the prophecy, when it is said:

They shall deny. They shall abominate and hate, like Balac, who asked Balaam to drive back the Israelites by cursing them.

504BThou shalt tread. By subjecting to the Church those who abominate and persecute her.

It is to be noted that there are only eleven blessings. For Simeon is not found among these blessings, in whom Judas is excluded from the apostles, only eleven remaining. Thence also by eleven encampments the sons of Israel came from Horeb to the place in which they received Deuteronomy.

CAPUT XXXIV

VERS. 1.-- Ascendit ergo. Tam Moyses quam caeteri patres Veteris Testamenti per spiritum in altitudinem contemplationis levati, praevidere potuerunt gratiam Novi Testamenti, quae in Christo data est, et virtutum collatione et coelestis regni perceptione: 504Csed eam in carne exspectare non potuerunt, in fide autem et spe mortui sunt, ad inferos deducti, ubi exspectabant Redemptoris adventum, qui per sanguinem suae redemptionis inde eos liberaret.

Ostenditque ei. Terram promissionis vidit Moyses, nec intravit, quia lex adventum Christi et doctrinam Evangelii verbis et figuris praesignavit, sed neminem ad perfectum duxit.

VERS. 2.-- Omnem terram. Partem omnis terrae, quam ex altissimo monte potuit videre. Vel omnem terram ostendit ei in spiritu.

VERS. 5.-- Mortuusque est ibi, etc. Nulli electorum parcit, et minima quoque peccata ulciscitur Deus etiam in amicis; unde: Moyses et Aaron sacerdotibus ejus, et addidit: Propitius fuisti eis, et 504Dulciscens in omnes adinventiones eorum. Lege scilicet finita, quae servivit Domino in tempore suo, urbs destructa et altare, nusquam sacrificia, nusquam libamina, nusquam sacerdos vel pontifex.

VERS. 6.-- In valle. In monte mortuus est, in valle sepultus est, quia lex tempore suo gloriosa fuit: sed comparata Evangelio abscondita videtur et humilis; unde: Dedi eis praecepta non bona.

Et non cognovit homo. Judaei autumant hoc factum, ne Judaei semper ad idololatriam proni mortuum colerent tanquam Deum; sed secundum allegoriam tempus et modum finiendi legis in sola Dei praescientia significat esse abscondita, nec profunditatem sapientiae Dei, vel secretum consilii alicui 505Apatere, quia incomprehensibilia judicia ejus, et investigabiles viae ejus.

VERS. 7.-- Moyses centum et viginti annorum. Si ab uno per naturalem ordinem usque ad quindecim singulorum numerorum summas conjunxeris, unum scilicet et duo et tria, et deinceps, centum et viginti fiunt. Quindenarius vero ex septem et octo conficitur, septem ad sabbatum refertur, octo ad circumcisionem, quia octava die circumcidebatur puer. Si vero ad Novum Testamentum respicias, habes septem dona Spiritus sancti, et in octavo resurrectionem Christi, centum et viginti ergo eos significant qui vel in Veteri Testamento Legis et Evangelii praecepta implere student.

Moyses centum et viginti annorum. Hoc significat tam eum quam caeteros patres Veteris Testamenti post perfectionem mandatorum Dei, quae est in duobus 505Bpraeceptis charitatis, per gratiam Christi et sanguinis redemptionem, aeternae quieti et futurae vitae aptos esse; quorum oculus non caligavit, piae scilicet intentionis intuitus nubilo non fuit obscuratus, nec dentes malitiae moti, quia non est perturbatus tempestate vitiorum, ordo discretionis eorum centum et viginti sunt anni legislatoris quo numero confirmata est altitudo Salomonici templi. Primitiva quoque Ecclesia post passionem et resurrectionem, Dominique ascensionem hoc numero virorum gratiam Spiritus sancti accepit. Quindecim namque qui ex septem et octo constant, futuram vitam significant quae nunc geritur in sabbato animarum et perficietur in resurrectione corporum. In trigonum quoque quindenarius ductus, id est cum partibus 505Csuis adnumeratus, efficit centum et viginti. Apte ergo centenario et vicenario electorum beatitudo in futura vita signatur, et tertium domus Dei coenaculum consummatur, quia post fidelium labores, post requiem animarum plena Ecclesiae felicitas in resurrectionis gloria complebitur.

506AVERS. 8.-- Fleveruntque. Quia regnavit mors etiam in eos qui non peccaverunt ab Adam usque ad Moysen: ideo lacrymae prosequuntur ad inferos descendentem.

(HIERON., epist. ad Paulam.) Non mirum si Moysi et Aaron, etc., usque ad sed intrinsecus esse, id est voluntati Domini ministrare.

Et completi. Hinc videtur orta consuetudo, ut fidelibus mortuis triginta diebus pietatis officia persolvantur.

VERS. 9.-- Posuit super eum. In Scripturis suis testimonium ei perhibuit dicens: Prophetam Deus suscitabit, etc. (Deut. XVIII), unde: Si crederetis Moysi, crederetis forsitan et mihi (Joan. V).

VERS. 10.-- Et non surrexit, etc. Hoc (ut fertur) Esdras de suo adjecit, qui bibliothecam a Chaldaeis exustam divino sensu reparavit, et litteras quibus Judaei nunc utuntur invenit, unde et velox scriba 506Bappellatus est.

Et non surrexit propheta. Quia Moyses omnibus prophetis Veteris Testamenti praestantior: unde Judaei arroganter dicunt: Nos Moysi discipuli sumus (Joan. IX). Sed postquam Dominus prophetarum incarnatus venit in mundum, omnis illa prior dignitas et umbra futurorum cessavit: quia lex et figura per Mosen data est: gratia et veritas per Jesum Christum, qui est finis legis ad justitiam omni credenti (Hebr. X; Joan. I; Rom. X).

Et sepelivit eum in valle. Scilicet Dominus. Et per hoc significatur quod amoto impedimento naturae per Christum anima munda separata ascendit ad Deum: et corpus quod sepelitur in terra remanet usque ad judicium. Ecclesiastes XII: Donec pulvis 506Crevertatur in pulverem unde erat, et spiritus redeat ad Deum qui dedit illum. Quem reditum prosperum nobis concedat, qui vivit et regnat in saecula saeculorum. Amen.

CHAPTER XXXIV

VERS. 1.-- He went up therefore. Moses as well as the other fathers of the Old Testament, lifted up in spirit to the height of contemplation, were able to foresee the grace of the New Testament, which is given in Christ, both by the conferring of the virtues and by the receiving of the heavenly kingdom: 504Cbut they could not await it in the flesh, and died in faith and hope, and were led down to the lower regions, where they awaited the coming of the Redeemer, who by the blood of his redemption should free them from thence.

And he showed him. Moses saw the land of promise, and did not enter it, because the law foreshadowed in words and figures the coming of Christ and the doctrine of the Gospel, but led no man to perfection.

VERS. 2.-- All the land. A part of all the land, so much as he could see from the highest mountain. Or he showed him all the land in spirit.

VERS. 5.-- And he died there, etc. God spares none of the elect, and avenges even the least sins, even in his friends; whence: Moses and Aaron his priests, and he added: Thou wast propitious to them, and 504Dtaking vengeance on all their inventions. The law, namely, being ended, which served the Lord in its own time, the city was destroyed and the altar, nowhere sacrifices, nowhere libations, nowhere priest or pontiff.

VERS. 6.-- In a valley. On the mountain he died, in a valley he was buried, because the law was glorious in its own time: but compared with the Gospel it seems hidden and lowly; whence: I gave them precepts that were not good.

And no man hath known. The Jews assert that this was done lest the Jews, ever prone to idolatry, should worship a dead man as God; but according to allegory it signifies that the time and manner of the ending of the law are hidden in the foreknowledge of God alone, and that neither the depth of the wisdom of God, nor the secret of his counsel, lies open to anyone, 505Abecause his judgments are incomprehensible and his ways unsearchable.

VERS. 7.-- Moses was a hundred and twenty years old. If from one, in the natural order, as far as fifteen, thou shalt join together the sums of the single numbers — one, that is, and two and three, and so onward — they make a hundred and twenty. And fifteen is made up of seven and eight: seven is referred to the sabbath, eight to circumcision, because on the eighth day the boy was circumcised. But if thou look to the New Testament, thou hast the seven gifts of the Holy Spirit, and in the eighth the resurrection of Christ; the hundred and twenty therefore signify those who even in the Old Testament strive to fulfil the precepts of the Law and of the Gospel.

Moses was a hundred and twenty years old. This signifies that he, as well as the other fathers of the Old Testament, after the perfection of the commandments of God, which is in the two 505Bprecepts of charity, are through the grace of Christ and the redemption of his blood made fit for eternal rest and for the life to come; whose eye was not dimmed, the gaze, namely, of holy intention was not darkened with any cloud, nor were the teeth of malice moved, because their order of discretion was not disturbed by the tempest of the vices, a hundred and twenty are the years of the lawgiver, by which number the height of Solomon's temple was confirmed. The primitive Church also, after the passion and the resurrection and the Lord's ascension, received the grace of the Holy Spirit in this number of men. For fifteen, which is made up of seven and eight, signifies the life to come, which is now carried on in the sabbath of souls and shall be perfected in the resurrection of bodies. Fifteen also, brought into a triangle, that is, reckoned together with its own parts, 505Cmakes a hundred and twenty. Fittingly therefore is the beatitude of the elect in the life to come signified by the hundred and the twenty, and the third upper room of the house of God is completed, because after the labours of the faithful, after the rest of souls, the full felicity of the Church shall be fulfilled in the glory of the resurrection.

506AVERS. 8.-- And they wept. Because death reigned even in those who had not sinned, from Adam unto Moses: therefore tears follow him as he goes down to the lower regions.

(HIERON., epist. ad Paulam.) It is no wonder if to Moses and Aaron, etc., as far as but to be within, that is, to minister to the will of the Lord.

And they were completed. From this the custom seems to have arisen, that for the faithful departed the offices of piety are discharged for thirty days.

VERS. 9.-- He laid upon him. In his Scriptures he bore witness to him, saying: God shall raise up a prophet, etc. (Deut. XVIII), whence: If you did believe Moses, you would perhaps believe me also (Joan. V).

VERS. 10.-- And there arose not, etc. This (as is reported) Esdras added of his own, who by a divine understanding restored the library burnt by the Chaldeans, and found the letters which the Jews now use, whence he was also called a swift scribe 506B.

And there arose not a prophet. Because Moses was more excellent than all the prophets of the Old Testament: whence the Jews arrogantly say: We are the disciples of Moses (Joan. IX). But after the Lord of the prophets came incarnate into the world, all that former dignity and shadow of things to come ceased: because the law and the figure was given by Moses: grace and truth by Jesus Christ, who is the end of the law unto justice to everyone that believeth (Hebr. X; Joan. I; Rom. X).

And he buried him in the valley. The Lord, namely. And by this it is signified that, the hindrance of nature having been removed through Christ, the clean soul, once separated, ascends to God: and the body which is buried remains in the earth until the judgment. Ecclesiastes XII: Until the dust 506Creturn into the dust whence it was, and the spirit return to God who gave it. Which return may he grant to be prosperous for us, who liveth and reigneth for ever and ever. Amen.

Parenthetical references in small type stand on Migne's page — editorial identifications inherited from the edition he reprinted or supplied by his shop, not necessarily the author's own; the author's own citations run in the prose itself.
